\hline\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0ptb$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle f$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle c$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle c$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right)$}}\right\}\cap G_{2n}.

Note that
S_{k}^{\prime}
and
M_{\psi_{k}}^{\prime}
are subgroups of
M_{r,2n-r}
and moreover
S_{k}^{\prime}=M_{\psi_{k}^{\prime}}\ltimes N_{k}^{\prime}.

4.3.4.
The subgroups
N(k,1)
and
N(k,2)

Finally, let
N(k,1)
and
N(k,2)
be subgroups of
G_{r}
and
G_{2n-r}
respectively defined by

(4.27)

N(k,1)=\left\{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}I_{k}&x&y\\
0&I_{k}&0\\
0&0&I_{r-2k}\end{array}\right]:x\in\mathcal{M}_{k},y\in\mathcal{M}_{k,r-2k}\right\}

and

(4.28)

N(k,2)=\left\{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}I_{r-2k}&0&w\\
0&I_{n-r+k}&z\\
0&0&I_{n-r+k}\end{array}\right]:w\in\mathcal{M}_{r-2k,n-r+k},z\in\mathcal{M}_{n-r+k}\right\}.

4.3.5.
The characters
\psi_{k,1}
and
\psi_{k,2}

Let
\psi_{k,1}
be the character of
N(k,1)
defined by

(4.29)

\psi_{k,1}(n_{1})=\psi_{0}({\rm tr}(x)).

Similarly, let
\psi_{k,2}
be the character of
N(k,2)
defined by

(4.30)

\psi_{k,2}(n_{2})=\psi_{0}({\rm tr}(z)).

Then, we have
N_{k}^{\prime}=N(k,1)\times N(k,2)\mbox{ and }\psi^{\prime}_{k}({\rm diag}(n_{1},n_{2}))=\psi_{k,1}(n_{1})\psi_{k,2}(n_{2}),
i.e.,
\psi^{\prime}_{k}=\psi_{k,1}\times\psi_{k,2}.

Remark 4.11
.

We note that the subgroup
N(k,1)
is indeed the subgroup
N_{k,r-k},
the unipotent radical of the parabolic subgroup
P_{k,r-k}
of
G_{r}.
However, we have preferred to write it in steps of three blocks to highlight the square block
x\in\mathcal{M}_{k}
which contributes to the character
\psi_{k,1}.
A similar remark applies to the subgroup
N(k,2)
as well, which is the unipotent radical
N_{n-k,n-r+k}
of the parabolic subgroup
P_{n-k,n-r+k}
of
G_{2n-r}.

4.3.6.

We need the following simple lemma.

Lemma 4.12
.

Suppose
(\rho,V)\in{\rm{Alg}}(P_{r,2n-r})
acts trivially on
N_{r,2n-r}.
Let
N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime},N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime}
be defined as in Section
4.1
. The following statements hold:

(1)

The subgroup
M_{\psi_{k}^{\prime}}
of
M_{r,2n-r}
normalizes
N_{k}^{\prime}
and the character
\psi_{k}^{\prime}.

(2)

V_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}=V_{N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime}}
as
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces.

(3)

For
v\in V
and
\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&C^{\prime}\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\in\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}^{\prime\prime},

(4.31)

\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&C^{\prime}\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}(v+V(N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}))=\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&0\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}(v+V(N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime})).

Proof.

The statement (1) is easily verified. As
V(N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime})=V(N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime})
as
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces, (2) follows. Next, we compare the action of
\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\in{\rm{Alg}}(\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k})
with that of
\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime}}\in{\rm{Alg}}(M_{\psi_{k}^{\prime}}).
For
v\in V,
noting that the representation
\rho
acts trivially on
N_{r,2n-r},
we get

\displaystyle\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&C^{\prime}\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}(v+V(N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}))

\displaystyle=\rho\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&C^{\prime}\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}(v)+V(N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\rho\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&0\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}(v)+V(N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&0\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}(v+V(N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime})).\qed

4.3.7.

We are ready to state the structure theorem in its final form.

Theorem 4.13
.

Fix an integer
r
such that
1\leq r<2n.

For
\rho_{1}\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{r})
and
\rho_{2}\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{2n-r})
put
\rho:=\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}
and
\pi=i_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{G_{2n}}(\rho).
Let
\psi
be the character
N
defined by (
1.1
). Put
\alpha=\max\{0,r-n\}
and
\beta={\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}.
Then,
\pi_{N,\psi}
has a filtration

(4.32)

\{0\}\subset V_{\alpha}\subset\dots\subset V_{\beta}=\pi_{N,\psi},

such that for
\alpha\leq k\leq\beta
we have an isomorphism of
\Delta G_{n}
-modules given by

(4.33)

V_{k}/V_{k-1}\cong{\rm{ind}}_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{\Delta G_{n}}\left(\tau_{k}\otimes[\delta_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{1/2}]^{w_{k}}\otimes\delta_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{-1}\right)

where

(4.34)

\tau_{k}\left(\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left(\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-6.66669pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle a$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle d$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle e$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle b$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle f$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle c$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
\hline\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pta$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle d$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle e$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle b$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle f$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle c$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right)$}}\right)

=(\rho_{1})_{N(k,1),\psi_{k,1}}\left(\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left(\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-6.66669pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle a$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle a$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle d$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle b$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right)$}}\right)\otimes(\rho_{2})_{N(k,2),\psi_{k,2}}\left(\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left(\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-6.66669pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle b$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle f$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle c$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle c$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right)$}}\right).

Proof.

By Theorem
4.9
,

V_{k}/V_{k-1}\cong{\rm{ind}}_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{\Delta G_{n}}\left([\rho_{{}_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}}\otimes\delta_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{1/2}]^{w_{k}}\otimes\delta_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{-1}\right).

It is trivial that
[\rho_{{}_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}}\otimes\delta_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{1/2}]^{w_{k}}=[\rho_{{}_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}}]^{w_{k}}\otimes[\delta_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{1/2}]^{w_{k}}.
Write an element
Z\in P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}
as

Z=\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left(\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-6.66669pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle a$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle d$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle e$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle b$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle f$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle c$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right)$}}.

Then, by (
4.24
)

[\rho_{{}_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}}]^{w_{k}}\begin{pmatrix}Z&0\\
0&Z\end{pmatrix}(v+V(N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}))=\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&C^{\prime}\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}(v+V(N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime})),

where

A^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}a&0&0\\
0&a&d\\
0&0&b\end{pmatrix}\in G_{r},B^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}b&f&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&c\end{pmatrix}\in G_{2n-r}
and

C^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}d&e&0\\
0&0&e\\
0&0&f\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{M}_{r,2n-r}.

Appealing to Lemma
4.12
, one has

\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&C^{\prime}\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}(v+V(N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}))=\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&0\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\right)(v+V(N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime})).

Applying Lemma
2.2
,
we obtain
\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime}}=(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})_{N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime}}=(\rho_{1})_{N(k,1),\psi_{k,1}}\otimes(\rho_{2})_{N(k,2),\psi_{k,2}}

as
{\rm Norm}_{G_{r}}(N(k,1),\psi_{k,1})\times{\rm Norm}_{G_{2n-r}}(N(k,2),\psi_{k,2})
-modules and hence as
M_{\psi_{k}^{\prime}}
-modules by Lemma
4.12
(1).
Therefore,

\displaystyle[\rho_{{}_{N_{k}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime\prime}}}]^{w_{k}}\begin{pmatrix}Z&0\\
0&Z\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\rho_{N_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&0\\
0&B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=(\rho_{1})_{N(k,1),\psi_{k,1}}(A^{\prime})\otimes(\rho_{2})_{N(k,2),\psi_{k,2}}(B^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau_{k}\begin{pmatrix}Z&0\\
0&Z\end{pmatrix}.

This completes the proof of the theorem. ∎

4.3.8.

One immediately has the following result which is a ‘component wise’ version of Theorem
4.6
. For the following corollary, we shall keep the notations as in Theorem
4.13
.

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
\rho_{1}\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{r}),\rho_{2}\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{2n-r})
and put
\pi=i_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{G_{2n}}(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}).
Then,
\pi_{N,\psi}
is non-zero if and only if there exists an integer
k
such that
\alpha\leq k\leq\beta
for which both the twisted Jacquet modules
(\rho_{1})_{N(k,1),\psi_{k,1}}
and
(\rho_{2})_{N(k,2),\psi_{k,2}}
are simultaneously non-zero.

4.3.9.

We shall also state below a normalized version of Theorem
4.13
, (i.e., where all the inductions and twisted Jacquet functors are normalized) which may be more useful in applications.

Corollary 4.15
.

Keeping notations and assumptions as in Theorem
4.13
,
\pi_{{N,\psi}}
has a filtration

\{0\}\subset V_{\alpha}\subset\dots\subset V_{\beta}=\pi{{}_{N,\psi}}

where for
\alpha\leq k\leq\beta,
we have an isomorphism of
\Delta G_{n}
-modules given by

(4.35)

V_{k}/V_{k-1}\cong i_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{\Delta G_{n}}\left(\tau_{k}^{\prime}\otimes[\delta_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{1/2}]^{w_{k}}\otimes\delta_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{-3/2}\right)

where

(4.36)

\tau_{k}^{\prime}\left(\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left(\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-6.66669pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle a$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle d$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle e$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle b$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle f$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle c$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
\hline\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pta$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle d$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle e$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle b$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle f$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle n-r+k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt\vrule$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern-5.0pt0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle c$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right)$}}\right)

=\nu^{\frac{r-2k}{2}}(a)\nu^{-\frac{k}{2}}(b)r_{N(k,1),\psi_{k,1}}(\rho_{1})\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&0&0\\
0&a&d\\
0&0&b\end{pmatrix}\right)\otimes\nu^{\frac{n-r+k}{2}}(b)\nu^{\frac{2k-r}{2}}(c)r_{N(k,2),\psi_{k,2}}(\rho_{2})\left(\begin{pmatrix}b&f&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&c\end{pmatrix}\right).

Proof.

By Theorem
4.13
, one has

(4.37)

V_{k}/V_{k-1}\cong{\rm{ind}}_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{\Delta G_{n}}\left(\tau_{k}\otimes[\delta_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{1/2}]^{w_{k}}\otimes\delta_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{-1}\right)

where
\tau_{k}
is as in (
4.34
).
Since, for any
\ell
-group
G,
a closed subgroup
H
of
G
and
\eta\in{\rm{Alg}}(H),

i_{H}^{G}(\eta)={\rm{ind}}_{H}^{G}(\delta_{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta_{G}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\eta)
, we get

(4.38)

V_{k}/V_{k-1}\cong i_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{\Delta G_{n}}\left(\tau_{k}\otimes[\delta_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{1/2}]^{w_{k}}\otimes\delta_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{-3/2}\right).

Note that
(\rho_{i})_{N(k,i),\psi_{k,i}}\simeq{\rm mod}_{N(k,i)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes r_{N(k,i),\psi_{k,i}}(\rho_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}.
To establish the corollary, we need to compute the action of the modular characters
{\rm mod}_{N(k,1)}
and
{\rm mod}_{N(k,2)}
on the subgroups
M_{\psi_{k}}^{\prime}\cap G_{r}
and
M_{\psi_{k}}^{\prime}\cap G_{2n-r}
respectively.
Let
f_{0}
denote a locally constant compactly supported function on
N(k,1).
For

A=\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left(\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-6.66669pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle a$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle a$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle d$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle r-2k$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle b$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right)$}}\in M_{\psi_{k}^{\prime}}\cap G_{r}
we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle N(k,1)$}}$}}\nolimits f_{0}(A^{-1}n_{1}A)dn_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle N(k,1)$}}$}}\nolimits\begin{pmatrix}I_{k}&a^{-1}xa&a^{-1}yb+a^{-1}xd\\
0&I_{k}&0\\
0&0&I_{r-2k}\end{pmatrix}dxdy.

Applying a succession of change of variables in the order
x\to axa^{-1},y\to ayb^{-1}-xdb^{-1},
we get

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle N(k,1)$}}$}}\nolimits f_{0}(A^{-1}n_{1}A)dn_{1}

\displaystyle=\nu(a)^{r-2k}\nu^{-k}(b)\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle N(k,1)$}}$}}\nolimits\begin{pmatrix}I_{k}&x&y\\
0&I_{k}&0\\
0&0&I_{r-2k}\end{pmatrix}dxdy

Therefore,
{\rm mod}_{N(k,1)}\left(A\right)=\nu^{2k-r}(a)\nu^{-k}(b).
By a similar calculation,

{\rm mod}_{N(k,2)}\left(\begin{pmatrix}b&f&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&c\end{pmatrix}\right)=\nu^{n-r+k}(b)\nu^{2k-r}(c).

The corollary follows.
∎

4.4.
Structure of the twisted Jacquet module in the finite field case

4.4.1.

In this section, we shall prove our main structure theorem for a parabolically induced representation of
GL_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}).
To begin with, we note some standard facts on representations of finite groups. Let
G
be a finite group and
(\pi,V)
be a finite dimensional complex representation of
G.
For a subgroup
H
of
G
and a character
\chi
of
H,
we have

V(H,\chi):={\rm Span}\{\pi(h)v-\chi(h)v:v\in V,h\in H\}=\{v\in V:\sum_{h\in H}\chi^{-1}(h)\pi(h)v=0\}.

Let

V^{H,\chi}:=\{v\in V:\pi(h)v=\chi(h)v\>\text{for all}\>h\in H\}.

4.4.2.

Let
S
be a subgroup of
G
which normalizes both
H
and
\chi.
Then,
V^{H,\chi}
is an
S
-invariant subspace of
V
and becomes a representation of
S
which we shall denote by
\pi^{H,\chi}.
Also, denote the restriction of
\pi
to
V(H,\chi)
by
\pi(H,\chi).
We then have
\pi=\pi(H,\chi)\bigoplus\pi^{H,\chi}
and consequently, obtain an isomorphism of
S
-modules:

(4.39)

\pi_{H,\chi}\simeq\pi^{H,\chi}.

4.4.3.

One of the main ingredients in the proof of Theorem
4.9
was Proposition
4.1
which was proved using Theorem
2.3
. Here, we prove the analogue of Proposition
4.1
in the finite group case directly giving a standard argument.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
G_{0}
be a finite group. Let
G,U
be subgroups of
G_{0}
and let
\theta:U\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a character such that
G
normalizes both
U
and
\theta.
Let
H
be a subgroup of
G
and
U_{0}
a subgroup of
U.
Let
\theta_{0}
denote the restriction of
\theta
to
U_{0}.
Assume further that
H
normalizes
U_{0}
and
\theta_{0}.
For any representation
(\rho,V)
of the group
HU_{0},
we have an isomorphism of
G
-modules:

(4.40)

({\rm{Ind}}_{HU_{0}}^{GU}(\rho))_{U,\theta}\simeq{\rm{Ind}}_{H}^{G}(\rho_{U_{0},\theta_{0}})

Proof.

In view of the isomorphism (
4.39
), to prove (
4.40
), it suffices to show that
({\rm{Ind}}_{HU_{0}}^{GU}(\rho))^{U,\theta}
is equivalent to
{\rm{Ind}}_{H}^{G}(\rho^{U_{0},\theta_{0}})
as
G
-modules. To this end, let
\phi:({\rm{Ind}}_{HU_{0}}^{GU}(\rho))^{U,\theta}\to{\rm{Ind}}_{H}^{G}(\rho^{U_{0},\theta_{0}})
denote the map obtained by restricting a function
f\in({\rm{Ind}}_{HU_{0}}^{GU}(\rho))^{U,\theta}
to
G.
For the purposes of this proof, we shall denote the action of
{\rm{Ind}}_{HU_{0}}^{GU}(\rho)
by
\sigma.
We will show that
\phi
is well defined and is a
G
-equivariant linear bijection.

To show that
\phi
is well defined, we show first that
\phi(f)(g)\in\rho^{U_{0},\theta_{0}}
for every
g\in G.
For
u_{0}\in U_{0},

\rho(u_{0})(\phi(f)(g))=\rho(u_{0})f(g)=f(u_{0}g).
Since
G
normalizes
U,
let
u_{1}\in U
be such that
u_{0}g=gu_{1}.
Then,
f(u_{0}g)=f(gu_{1})=(\sigma(u_{1})f)(g)=(\theta(u_{1})f)(g)=\theta(u_{1})f(g)=\theta(u_{0})f(g).
Thus,
\phi(f)(g)\in\rho^{U_{0},\theta_{0}}.
Next, take
h\in H
and
g\in G.
Note that
\phi(f)(hg)=f(hg)=\rho(h)(f(g)).
So,
\phi(f)\in{\rm{Ind}}_{H}^{G}(\rho^{U_{0},\theta_{0}}).
It is obvious that
\phi
is linear and straightforward to check that
\phi
is a
G
-equivariant map.

To show injectivity of
\phi,
suppose that
\phi(f)=0,
i.e.,
f(g)=0
for every
g\in G.
Take
u\in U,g\in G.
Since
f\in({\rm{Ind}}_{HU_{0}}^{GU}(\rho))^{U,\theta},
we see that
f(gu)=(\sigma(u)f)(g)=\theta(u)f(g)=0,
proving
\phi
is injective.

Let
f^{\prime}\in{\rm{Ind}}_{H}^{G}(\rho^{U_{0},\theta_{0}}).
Define
f:GU\to V
by
f(gu)=\theta(u)f^{\prime}(g).
Then, for
h\in H,u_{0}\in U_{0},g\in G,u\in U
we obtain
f(hu_{0}gu)=f(hgu_{0}^{\prime}u)
where
u_{0}^{\prime}
is such that
u_{0}g=gu_{0}^{\prime}.
Then,
\theta(u_{0})=\theta(u_{0}^{\prime})
as
G
normalizes
\theta.
In view of this,

f(hgu_{0}^{\prime}u)=\theta(u_{0}^{\prime}u)f^{\prime}(hg)=\theta(u_{0}^{\prime}u)\rho(h)(f^{\prime}(g))=\theta(u_{0})\theta(u)\rho(h)(f^{\prime}(g)).

Now,
\theta(u_{0})\theta(u)\rho(h)(f^{\prime}(g))=\theta(u)\rho(h)(\theta(u_{0})f^{\prime}(g))=\theta(u)\rho(h)(\rho(u_{0})(f^{\prime}(g)))=\rho(hu_{0})f(gu).
Also, if
u\in U,u^{\prime}\in U
and
g\in G,
we have
[\sigma(u)f](gu^{\prime})=f(gu^{\prime}u)=\theta(u^{\prime}u)f^{\prime}(g)=\theta(u)\theta(u^{\prime})f^{\prime}(g)=\theta(u)f(gu^{\prime}).
We have shown that
f\in{\rm{Ind}}_{HU_{0}}^{GU}(\rho))^{U,\theta}
and
\phi(f)=f^{\prime}
proving
\phi
is surjective as well, completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 4.17
.

With Proposition
4.16
in place, it is straightforward to deduce the analogues of Corollary
4.3
, Theorem
4.6
and Theorem
4.9
for
GL_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}).
We need to keep in mind that all modular characters are trivial in this case and instead of a filtration as in Theorem
4.9
, one has a direct sum in the case of
GL_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}).
Note also that to prove the analogue of Theorem
4.9
, we need only invoke the finite field analogue of Theorem
3.4
.

4.4.4.
Structure theorem in the finite field case

We state the main result in the finite field case which is the analogue of Theorem
4.13
.
Keeping Remark
4.17
in mind, the proof of Theorem
4.18
which we omit, follows by the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
4.13
. In the next theorem,
G_{n}=GL_{n}(F)
where
F
is a finite field with
q
elements.

Theorem 4.18
.

Fix an integer
r
such that
1\leq r<2n.

Let
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
be finite dimensional representations of
G_{r}
and
G_{2n-r}
respectively. Put
\pi={\rm{Ind}}_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{G_{2n}}(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}).
Fix a non trivial additive character
\psi_{0}
of
F.
Let
\psi
be the character of
N
defined by (
1.1
). Let
N(k,1)
and
N(k,2)
be the subgroups of
G_{r}
and
G_{2n-r}
respectively defined by (
4.27
) and (
4.28
). Also, let
\psi_{k,1}
and
\psi_{k,2}
be the characters of
N(k,1)
and
N(k,2)
defined by (
4.29
) and (
4.30
).
Then,

\pi_{N,\psi}=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\max\{0,r-n\}\leq k\leq{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}}{\rm{Ind}}_{\Delta P_{k,r-2k,n-r+k}}^{\Delta G_{n}}(\tau_{k})

where
\tau_{k}\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&d&e&0&0&0\\
0&b&f&0&0&0\\
0&0&c&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&a&d&e\\
0&0&0&0&b&f\\
0&0&0&0&0&c\end{pmatrix}\right)=(\rho_{1})_{N(k,1),\psi_{k,1}}\begin{pmatrix}a&0&0\\
0&a&d\\
0&0&b\end{pmatrix}\otimes(\rho_{2})_{N(k,2),\psi_{k,2}}\begin{pmatrix}b&f&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&c\end{pmatrix}.

5.
Some special cases and applications

The aim of this section is to consider a few special cases of the computation of the twisted Jacquet module and illustrate a few applications of our results. Throughout the rest of the article, we fix
F
to be a non-archimedean local field. If
\rho_{i}\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{n_{i}})
for
i\in\{1,\dots,s\},
and
n=\sum n_{i}
then
\rho_{1}\times\dots\times\rho_{s}
will denote the parabolically induced representation
i_{P_{n_{1},\dots,n_{s}}}^{G_{n}}(\rho_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\rho_{s}).
In Section
5.1
, we compute
\pi_{N,\psi}
where
\pi
is a representation of
G_{2n}
of the form
\chi\times\rho
or
\rho\times\chi
where
\rho
is any smooth representation and
\chi
is any character. Section
5.2
discusses certain instances where the twisted Jacquet module of a principal series representation is non-zero. We determine the structure of twisted Jacquet modules in Section
5.3
of certain irreducible representations of
G_{2n}
which are subquotients of certain principal series representations. As a consequence, we show the existence of Shalika model for certain non-generic irreducible representation representation of
G_{2n}.
In Section
5.4
, we present the structure of twisted Jacquet modules of subquotients of
St_{r}.\chi\times\mu,
where
\mu
and
\chi
are characters. In Sections
5.5
and
5.6
, we consider two particular reducible principal series representations of
G_{4}
and determine precisely for which subquotients of these reducible principal series the twisted Jacquet module is non-vanishing.

5.1.
Product of a character with a smooth representation

5.1.1.

In this subsection, we shall consider the parabolic induction of a character with a smooth representation. The structure of the twisted Jacquet module of such a representation is relatively simple. In particular, if
\chi
and
\mu
are one characters of
G_{r}
and
G_{2n-r},
then we show that
(\chi\times\mu)_{N,\psi}\neq 0
if and only if
r=n
and in which case
(\chi\times\mu)_{N,\psi}=\chi\otimes\mu.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
r
be such that
1\leq r<2n.
Let
\chi
be a character of
G_{r},

\rho\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{2n-r})
and put
\pi=i_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{G_{2n}}(\chi\otimes\rho).

(1)

If
r>n,
then
\pi_{N,\psi}=0,

(2)

If
r=n,

\pi_{N,\psi}=\chi\otimes\rho
as
\Delta G_{n}
-modules, and

(3)

If
r<n,
then
\pi_{N,\psi}\simeq i_{\Delta P_{r,n-r}}^{\Delta G_{n}}(\tau\otimes[\delta_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{1/2}]^{w_{0}}\otimes\delta_{P_{r,n-r}}^{-3/2})
where
\tau
acts on the group
\Delta P_{r,2n-r}
as follows:

(5.1)

\tau\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{pmatrix}b&f\\
0&c\end{pmatrix}&\\
&\begin{pmatrix}b&f\\
0&c\end{pmatrix}\end{array}\right]\right)\simeq\chi(b)\otimes\nu(b)^{\frac{n-r}{2}}\nu(c)^{-\frac{r}{2}}r_{N_{2},\psi_{2}}(\rho_{2})\left(\begin{pmatrix}b&f&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&c\end{pmatrix}\right),

where
N_{2}=\left\{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}I_{r}&0&w\\
0&I_{n-r}&z\\
0&0&I_{n-r}\end{array}\right]\right\}
and
\psi_{2}(n)=\psi_{0}(tr(z))
for
n\in N_{2}.

Proof.

Recall that
\alpha=\max\{0,r-n\}
and
\beta=\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor.
By Theorem
4.13
,
\pi_{N,\psi}
is glued from the representations
V_{k}/V_{k-1}
(see (
4.35
) and (
4.36
)) where
\alpha\leq k\leq\beta.
We note that for any
k>0,
the subgroup
N(k,1)
and the character
\psi_{k,1}
in Corollary
4.15
are non-trivial whereas the character
\chi
acts trivially on
N(k,1).
Thus, for any
k>0,

r_{N(k,1),\psi_{k,1}}(\chi)=0.
If
r>n,
we have
\alpha=r-n>0
and therefore, the range of values that
k
assumes is always positive, forcing
\pi_{N,\psi}=0.
This proves (i).

On the other hand, if
r\leq n,
we have
\alpha=0.
By the argument noted above, the only
V_{k}/V_{k-1}
which can be non-zero is
V_{0}
i.e., when the subgroup
N(k,1)
is trivial. The statements (ii) and (iii) of the corollary follow by putting
k=0
in (
4.35
) and (
4.36
). ∎

5.1.2.

We next obtain a proposition analogous to the previous one by interchanging the roles of
\rho
and
\chi.
We omit the proof.

Proposition 5.2
.

Fix an
r
such that
1\leq r<2n.
Let
\rho\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{r})
and
\chi
be a character of
G_{2n-r}.
Put
\pi=i_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{G_{2n}}(\rho\otimes\chi).
Then,

(1)

if
r<n,\pi_{N,\psi}=0,

(2)

if
r=n,

\pi_{N,\psi}=\rho\otimes\chi
as
\Delta G_{n}
-modules, and

(3)

if
r>n,
then
\pi_{N,\psi}\simeq i_{\Delta P_{r-n,2n-r}}^{\Delta G_{n}}(\tau\otimes[\delta_{P_{r,2n-r}}^{1/2}]^{w_{r-n}}\otimes\delta_{P_{r-n,2n-r}}^{-3/2})
where
\tau
acts on the group
\Delta P_{r-n,2n-r}
as follows:

(5.2)

\tau\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{pmatrix}a&d\\
0&b\end{pmatrix}&\\
&\begin{pmatrix}a&d\\
0&b\end{pmatrix}\end{array}\right]\right)\simeq\nu(a)^{\frac{2n-r}{2}}\nu(b)^{\frac{n-r}{2}}r_{N_{1},\psi_{1}}(\rho_{1})\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&0&0\\
0&a&d\\
0&0&b\end{pmatrix}\right)\otimes\chi(b),

where
N_{1}=\left\{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}I_{r-n}&x&y\\
0&I_{r-n}&0\\
0&0&I_{2n-r}\end{array}\right]\right\}
and
\psi_{1}(n)=\psi_{0}(tr(x))
for an element
n\in N_{1}.

5.1.3.

As a simple consequence of the two previous propositions, we single out the following special case.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\chi
and
\mu
be characters of
G_{r}
and
G_{2n-r}
respectively. Then,

(5.3)

(\chi\times\mu)_{N,\psi}\simeq\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }r\neq n\\
\chi\otimes\mu&\mbox{ if }r=n\end{cases}

as
\Delta G_{n}
-modules.

Proof.

By Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
, we have
(\chi\times\mu)_{N,\psi}=0
if
r>n
and
r<n
respectively. Hence,
(\chi\times\mu)_{N,\psi}
is non-zero only when
r=n,
in which case, the statement of the theorem follows from Proposition
5.1
(or Proposition
5.2
).
∎

Remark 5.4
.

The analogues of Propositions
5.1
,
5.2
hold if the respective groups are considered over
\mathbb{F}_{q}.
Thus, Theorem
5.3
holds over
\mathbb{F}_{q}.

5.2.
Certain Non-vanishing Twisted Jacquet Modules

5.2.1.

In this subsection, we shall give examples of certain classes of principal series representations with a non-zero twisted Jacquet module. To this end, let us note a generality. Suppose
G
is an
\ell
-group and
U_{1},U_{2}
are closed subgroups of
G
such that they are a union of their compact open subgroups. Assume further that
U_{1}\subset U_{2},

\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
are characters of
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
such that
{\theta_{2}}_{|U_{1}}=\theta_{1}.
Then, for any
\pi\in{\rm{Alg}}(G)
we have

(5.4)

{\rm{Hom}}_{U_{2}}(\pi,\theta_{2})\subset{\rm{Hom}}_{U_{1}}(\pi,\theta_{1}),

and therefore,
\pi_{U_{1},\theta_{1}}
is non-zero whenever
\pi_{U_{2},\theta_{2}}
is non-zero.

5.2.2.

We first have the following simple result.

Proposition 5.5
.

Suppose
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{n}).
Then,
(\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2})_{N,\psi}
is non-zero.

Proof.

We put
r=n
in Theorem
4.13
to get
\alpha=0.
The subgroups
N(0,1)
and
N(0,2)
are both
\{I_{n}\}
and hence
V_{0}=\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}
as
\Delta G_{n}
-modules. In particular,
V_{0}\subset(\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2})_{N,\psi}
yielding
(\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2})_{N,\psi}
is non-zero.
∎

5.2.3.

We recall the notion of a generic representation (
[
6
, §5.7]
,
[
15
, §2.3]
) of
G_{n}.
Let
U_{n}
denote the unipotent radical of the standard Borel subgroup
B_{n}
of
G_{n}.
One has a character
\theta
of
U_{n}
defined by
\theta(u)=\psi_{0}(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}u_{i,i+1})
for
u\in U_{n}.
An irreducible representation
\pi
of
G_{n}
is said to be generic if
{\rm{Hom}}_{U_{n}}(\pi,\theta)\neq 0.

5.2.4.

Note that the subgroup
N(1,1)
of
G_{n+1}
is contained in
U_{n+1}
and the character
\theta
of
U_{n+1}
restricted to
N(1,1)
coincides with
\psi_{1,1}.
Similarly, the subgroup
N(0,2)
of
G_{n+1}
is a subgroup of
U_{n+1}
and the character
\theta
restricted to
N(0,2)
coincides with
\psi_{0,2}.
By the generality noted in (
5.4
), we have proved

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\rho
be any irreducible generic representation of
G_{n+1}.
Then,
(\rho)_{N(1,1),\psi_{1,1}}
and
(\rho)_{N(0,2),\psi_{0,2}}
are both non-zero.

As a consequence, we have the following general result.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\rho\in{\rm{Irr}}(G_{n+1})
be generic and
\eta\in{\rm{Irr}}(G_{n-1})
be any representation. Then,

(1)

(\rho\times\eta)_{N,\psi}\neq 0
and,

(2)

(\eta\times\rho)_{N,\psi}\neq 0.

Proof.

We apply Theorem
4.13
(or Corollary
4.14
) with
r=n+1
to get
\alpha=1.
We shall show that
V_{1}
is non-zero (see (
4.32
) and (
4.33
)) by showing that
(\rho)_{N(1,1),\psi_{1,1}}
and
(\eta)_{N(1,2),\psi_{1,2}}
are both non-zero. We note that
N(1,2)
is the trivial subgroup
\{I_{n-1}\}
as
n-r+\alpha=0,
yielding
(\eta)_{N(1,2),\psi_{1,2}}=\eta.
On the other hand, as
\rho
is generic, by Lemma
5.6
,
(\rho)_{N(1,1),\psi_{1,1}}
is non zero, proving (1). The proof of (2) can be achieved similarly by applying Theorem
4.13
with
r=n-1
and proving that
V_{0}
is non-zero.
∎

5.2.5.
A general recipe

In view of Corollary
4.14
, in general, to construct a principal series
\pi=\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2}
of
G_{2n}
such that
\pi_{N,\psi}
is non-zero, it is sufficient to start with
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
such that
(\rho_{1})_{N(k,1),\psi_{k,1}}
and
(\rho_{2})_{N(k,2),\psi_{k,2}}
are simultaneously non-zero for some integer
k
satisfying
\max\{0,r-n\}\leq k\leq\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor.
We end this subsection with such a result for which our starting point is
[
19
]
.

5.2.6.
Connection with uneven Shalika Model

Consider the unipotent radical
U_{k}
of the parabolic
P_{k,k,r-2k}
of
G_{r}.
Put
H=\Delta G_{k}\times G_{r-2k}\subset M_{k,k,r-2k},
where
\Delta G_{k}
is the diagonal copy of
G_{k}
in
G_{k}\times G_{k}.
Then,
HU_{k}
is a subgroup of
P_{k,k,r-2k}.
Let
\theta
be any character of
HU_{k}
such that
\theta(u)=\psi_{0}(tr(x))
for each
u=\begin{pmatrix}I_{k}&x&y\\
0&I_{k}&z\\
0&0&I_{r-2k}\end{pmatrix}\in U_{k}.
Such a character is called a twisted Shalika character in
[
19
]
. If
\theta_{|H}=1,
then
\theta
is said to be a regular (non-twisted) Shalika character as well.

5.2.7.

It is shown in
[
19
]
that for any
\rho\in{\rm{Irr}}(G_{r})
,
{\rm{Hom}}_{G_{r}}(\rho,{\rm{Ind}}_{HU_{k}}^{G_{r}}(\theta))
has dimension at most one as a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space. If this space has dimension one, then
\rho
is said to have an uneven Shalika model. Representations with an uneven Shalika model thus forms a nice class of representations.

5.2.8.

Consider
\theta
as a character of the subgroup
U_{k}.
Note that
N(k,1)
is a subgroup of
U_{k}
and
\theta_{|N(k,1)}=\psi_{k,1}.
If
\rho\in{\rm{Irr}}(G_{r})
satisfies
\rho_{U_{k},\theta}\neq 0,
then
(\rho)_{N(k,1),\psi_{k,1}}\neq 0.

In a similar way, it is natural to consider the unipotent radical
U_{k}^{\prime}
of the parabolic subgroup
P_{r-2k,n-r+k,n-r+k}
of
G_{2n-r}.
For an element
u^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r-2k}&u&v\\
0&I_{n-r+k}&w\\
0&0&I_{n-r+k}\end{pmatrix}\in U_{k}^{\prime},
we may define
\theta^{\prime}(u^{\prime})=\psi_{0}(tr(w)).
If any irreducible representation
\eta
of
G_{2n-r}
satisfies
\eta_{U_{k}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}\neq 0,
then
(\eta)_{N(k,2),\psi_{k,2}}\neq 0.
We have obtained the following result.

Proposition 5.8
.

Suppose that
\rho\in{\rm{Irr}}(G_{r})
and
\eta\in{\rm{Irr}}(G_{2n-r}).
If the twisted Jacquet modules
(\rho)_{U_{k},\theta}
and
(\eta)_{U_{k}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}
are both non-zero for some
k
such that
\max\{0,r-n\}\leq k\leq\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor,
then
(\rho\times\eta)_{N,\psi}
is non-zero.

Remark 5.9
.

We shall return to the question of non-vanishing of twisted Jacquet modules in Sections
5.5
,
5.6
and in general in Section
6.3

5.3.
Applications

5.3.1.

We shall show how our results can be used to compute the structure of twisted Jacquet modules of certain irreducible representations of
G_{2n}
which are themselves not principal series representations but rather arise as subquotients of reducible principal series representations. We shall be using the notion of segments and multisegments and related results in our applications. We refer the reader to
[
31
]
for the notion of segments, multisegments and associated results. Also, see
[
15
]
for a summary of the main results.

5.3.2.

We briefly recall some notations. If
\Delta
is a segment
[
31
, §3]
, then by
Z(\Delta)
and
L(\Delta),
we mean the unique irreducible subrepresentation and the unique irreducible quotient associated to
\Delta.
In particular, if
\chi
is a character of
F^{\times}
and given any positive integer
r,
let
\Delta
denote the segment
[\chi\nu^{-\frac{r-1}{2}},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{r-1}{2}}].
Then,
Z(\Delta)
is the character
\chi
of
G_{r}
and
L(\Delta)
is the Steinberg representation
St_{r}
of
G_{r}.
A very useful result (
[
31
, §4.2 Theorem]
) which we shall use frequently is that a representation of the form
Z(\Delta_{1})\times\cdots\times Z(\Delta_{k})
is irreducible if and only if
\Delta_{i}
and
\Delta_{i}
are not linked for any
i,j
. An analogous statement holds (
[
31
, §9.7 Theorem]
) for representations of the form
L(\Delta_{1})\times\cdots\times L(\Delta_{k}).

5.3.3.

If
\mathfrak{m}=\{\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{r}\}
is a multisegment such that
\Delta_{i}
does not precede
\Delta_{j}
whenever
i<j,
by
Z(\mathfrak{m})
and
L(\mathfrak{m})
we mean the unique irreducible submodule and the unique irreducible quotient of the parabolically induced representations
Z(\Delta_{1})\times\cdots\times Z(\Delta_{r})
and
L(\Delta_{1})\times\cdots\times L(\Delta_{r})
respectively. We shall also use the Langlands Quotient theorem for
G_{n}
(see for instance
[
15
, Theorem 1.2.5 and Theorem 2.2.2]
). Since we shall be using the Moeglin-Waldspurger algorithm associated to Zelevinsky involution multiple times, we briefly recall the algorithm below from
[
1
]
and
[
17
]
.

5.3.4.
Zelevinsky Involution and the Moeglin-Waldspurger algorithm

Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a multisegment (that is a set of segments with multiplicities). The Moeglin-Waldspurger algorithm associates a multisegment
\mathfrak{m}^{t}
to
\mathfrak{m}
such that
Z(\mathfrak{m})=L(\mathfrak{m}^{t})
and vice-versa. We refer the reader to
[
17
, II.1 & II.2]
,
[
1
, §1]
and
[
2
, §2.8]
for more details.

In our work, we shall be considering only segments of the type
[\chi\nu^{a},\chi\nu^{a+1},\cdots,\chi\nu^{b}]
where
\chi
is a character of
F^{\times}.
Suppose
\Delta=[\chi\nu^{b},\chi\nu^{e}]
and
\Delta^{\prime}=[\chi\nu^{b^{\prime}},\chi\nu^{e^{\prime}}]
are segments where
\chi\in F^{\times}.

We say that
\Delta\geq\Delta^{\prime}
if
b>b^{\prime}
or
b=b^{\prime}
and
e\geq e^{\prime}.
To compute
\mathfrak{m}^{t},
the algorithm is as follows. Let
d
be the biggest ending of a segment in
\mathfrak{m}.
Choose a segment
\Delta_{i_{0}}
in
\mathfrak{m}
containing
d
and maximal for this property. Define integers
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}
inductively as follows:

\Delta_{i_{s}}
is a segment of
\mathfrak{m}
preceding
\Delta_{i_{s-1}}
with ending
d-s
maximal with these properties, and
r
is such that there is no possibility to find such an
i_{r+1}.
Let
\mathfrak{m}^{-}=(\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\Delta_{t}^{\prime})
where
\Delta_{i}^{\prime}=\Delta_{i}
if
i\notin\{i_{0},\dots,i_{r}\}
and
\Delta_{i}^{\prime}=\Delta_{i}^{-}
if
i\in\{i_{0},\dots,i_{r}\}.
Then
\{d-r,\dots,d\}
is the first segment of
\mathfrak{m}^{t}.
By repeating the process to
\mathfrak{m}^{-}
that was applied to
\mathfrak{m},
find the second segment of
\mathfrak{m}^{t}.
So
\mathfrak{m}^{t}
is the union of
\{d-r,\dots,d\}
and
(\mathfrak{m}^{-})^{t}.

5.3.5.

As a preparatory step, we summarize in the following lemma how the first step of Moeglin-Waldspurger algorithm works in some special cases so as to use them suited to our context.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\chi
be a character of
F^{\times}.

(1)

Suppose
\mathfrak{m}=\{\Delta\}
where
\Delta=[\chi\nu^{b},\dots,\chi\nu^{e}].
Then,
\mathfrak{m}^{t}=\{[\chi\nu^{e}],\dots,[\chi\nu^{b}]\}.

(2)

Assume that
\mathfrak{m}=\{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\}
where
\Delta_{1}=[\chi\nu^{b},\dots,\chi\nu^{e}]
and
\Delta_{2}=[\chi\nu^{b^{\prime}},\dots,\chi\nu^{e+1}]
where
b^{\prime}>b.
Then,
\mathfrak{m}^{t}
is the union of
[\chi\nu^{e},\chi\nu^{e+1}]
and
(\mathfrak{m}^{-})^{t}
where
\mathfrak{m}^{-}=\{\Delta_{1}^{-},\Delta_{2}^{-}\}.

(3)

Let
\mathfrak{m}=\{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\}
where
\Delta_{1}=[\chi\nu^{b},\dots,\chi\nu^{e}]
and
\Delta_{2}=[\chi\nu^{b^{\prime}},\dots,\chi\nu^{e^{\prime}}]
where
e^{\prime}>e+1
and
b^{\prime}>b.
Then,
\mathfrak{m}^{t}
is the union of
[\chi\nu^{e^{\prime}}]
and
(\mathfrak{m}^{-})^{t}
where
\mathfrak{m}^{-}=\{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}^{-}\}.

Proof.

The statement (1) is obvious. To prove (2), using the notations in the algorithm,
d=e+1
or equivalently
d-1=e.
Hence,
\Delta_{i_{0}}=\Delta_{2}
and
\Delta_{i_{1}}=\Delta_{1}.
Then,
\Delta_{1}^{-}=[\chi\nu^{b},\dots,\chi\nu^{e-1}]
and
\Delta_{2}^{-}=[\chi\nu^{b^{\prime}},\dots,\chi\nu^{e}],
by which
\mathfrak{m}^{-}=\{\Delta_{1}^{-},\Delta_{2}^{-}\},
proving (2). In the setting of (3),
d=e^{\prime}
and
d-1=e^{\prime}-1\neq e.
In this case, we get
\Delta_{i_{0}}=\Delta_{2},

\Delta_{2}^{-}=[\chi\nu^{b^{\prime}},\dots,\chi\nu^{e^{\prime}-1}],
and
\mathfrak{m}^{-}=\{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}^{-}\}.
Thus,
\mathfrak{m}^{t}
is the union of
[\chi\nu^{e^{\prime}}]
and
(\mathfrak{m}^{-})^{t},
which proves (3).
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\chi
be a character of
F^{\times}.
Put
\Delta=[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}}].
For each
\alpha\in\{1,\dots,n\}
put
\Delta_{\alpha}=[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\alpha}]
and
\xi_{\chi,\alpha}=Z(\Delta_{\alpha})\times Z(\Delta).
Then,
\xi_{\chi,\alpha}=\chi\nu^{\alpha}\times\chi
sits in the following exact sequence of
G_{2n}
-modules

(5.5)

0\to L_{\chi,\alpha}\to\xi_{\chi,\alpha}\to Z(\Delta\cup\Delta_{\alpha})\times Z(\Delta\cap\Delta_{\alpha})\ \to 0

where
L_{\chi,\alpha}
is the unique irreducible submodule of
\xi_{\chi,\alpha}.
If
1<\alpha\leq n,
put

\pi_{\chi,\alpha}:=\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\alpha}\times\cdots\times\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}+2}\times St_{2}\chi\nu^{\frac{n}{2}}\times\cdots\times St_{2}\chi\nu^{-\frac{n}{2}+\alpha}\times\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha-2}\times\cdots\times\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})},

and put

\pi_{\chi,1}=St_{2}\chi\nu^{\frac{n}{2}}\times St_{2}\chi\nu^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\times\cdots\times St_{2}\chi\nu^{-\frac{n}{2}+1}.

Then,
L_{\chi,\alpha}
is the unique irreducible quotient, and in particular the Langlands quotient, of the representation
\pi_{\chi,\alpha}
for
1\leq\alpha\leq n.

Proof.

The exact sequence (
5.5
) follows from
[
31
, Proposition 4.6]
(see also
[
30
, Lemma 2.2]
). Fix an
\alpha
and write
\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}=\{\Delta_{\alpha},\Delta\}.
We note that
\Delta
precedes
\Delta_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha
and hence
L_{\chi,\alpha}=Z(\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}).
In order to obtain the result, we apply Moeglin-Waldspurger algorithm to
\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}
and obtain a multisegment
\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}^{t}
so that we may write
L_{\chi,\alpha}=L(\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}^{t}).
We divide the proof into two cases.

Case 1
: If
\alpha=1,
we can directly apply Lemma
5.10
(2) to get that
\mathfrak{m}^{t}
is the union of
n
length
2
segments

[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+n-1},\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+n}],\dots,[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})},\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+1}].

Consequently,
L_{\chi,1}
is the Langlands quotient of
\pi_{\chi,1}.

Case 2
: Suppose
\alpha>1.
For
1\leq i\leq\alpha,
put

\Delta(i)=[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}+i}],\text{ and }\mathfrak{m}_{i}=\{\Delta(i),\Delta\}.

For
\alpha\leq j\leq n,
put

\Delta(j)^{\prime}=[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})},\dots,\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+j-1}]\text{ and }\Delta(j)^{\prime\prime}=[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha},\dots,\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+j}].

Also, note that
\alpha\geq 2
and put
\Delta_{\alpha-1}^{\prime}=[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})},\dots,\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha-2}]
and
\Delta_{\alpha-1}^{\prime\prime}
be the empty segment. Set

\mathfrak{m}_{j}^{\prime}:=\{\Delta(j)^{\prime},\Delta(j)^{\prime\prime}\}\text{ when }\alpha-1\leq j\leq n.

By Lemma
5.10
(3),
\mathfrak{m}_{i}^{t}
is the union of
\{[\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}+i}]\}
and
(\mathfrak{m}_{i}^{-})^{t}
for
2\leq i\leq\alpha.
One observes that
\mathfrak{m}_{i}^{-}=\mathfrak{m}_{i-1}.
Hence,
\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}^{t}
is the union of
\{[\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\alpha}],\dots,[\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}+2}]\}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{1}^{t}.
We note that
\mathfrak{m}_{1}=\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{\prime}
and determining
\mathfrak{m}_{1}^{t}
is the same as determining
(\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{\prime})^{t}.
Applying Lemma
5.10
(2), for
\alpha\leq j\leq n,

(\mathfrak{m}_{j}^{\prime})^{t}
is the union of
[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+j-1},\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+j}]
and
((\mathfrak{m}_{j}^{\prime})^{-})^{t}.
One observes that
(\mathfrak{m}_{j}^{\prime})^{-}=\mathfrak{m}_{j-1}^{\prime}.
Consequently,
(\mathfrak{m}_{j}^{\prime})^{t}
is the union of
[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+j-1},\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+j}]
and
(\mathfrak{m}_{j-1}^{\prime})^{t}
for
\alpha\leq j\leq n.
Recursively,
\mathfrak{m}_{1}^{t}
is the union of

\{[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+n},\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+n+1}],\dots,[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha-1},\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha}]\}

and
\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha-1}^{\prime}.
By Lemma
5.10
(1),
(\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha-1}^{\prime})^{t}=\{[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha-2}],\cdots,[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})}]\}.

We finally get
\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}^{t}
consists of the following:

(1)

\alpha-1
singleton segments
[\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\alpha}],\dots,[\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}+2}],

(2)

n-\alpha+1
segments
[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+n-1},\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+n}],\dots,[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha-1},\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha}],
of length
2
and

(3)

\alpha-1
singleton segments
[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})+\alpha-2}],\dots,[\chi\nu^{-(\frac{n-1}{2})}].

As
L([\chi\nu^{\beta},\chi\nu^{\beta+1}])=St_{2}\chi\nu^{\beta+1/2}
for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R},
it follows that
L_{\chi,\alpha}=L(\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}^{t})
is indeed the Langlands quotient of the representation
\pi_{\chi,\alpha}.

∎

Remark 5.12
.

We refer the reader to
[
2
, §4.1, Lemme 4.2]
to compare with Lemma
5.11
. Our lemma is a special case of the more general ‘ladder representations’ appearing there. As per the notations of Lemme 4.2 of
[
2
]
, in our case we have
N=2
and there must be
\frac{n-1}{2}+\alpha-(-\frac{n-1}{2})+N-2=n+\alpha-1
segments in
\mathfrak{m}^{t}.
In our description, if
\alpha>1
the number of segments in
\mathfrak{m}^{t}=2(\alpha-1)+n-\alpha+1=n+\alpha-1.
If
\alpha=1,
the same conclusion holds as we have
n-\alpha+1=n.

Remark 5.13
.

We note that the representations
L_{\chi,\alpha}
obtained in Corollary
5.11
are non-generic (see
[
31
, §9.8]
).

5.3.6.

As an immediate application of Lemma
5.11
, we can deduce the following result on the twisted Jacquet module of
L_{\chi,\alpha}.

Corollary 5.14
.

Keeping notations as in Lemma
5.11
, for
1\leq\alpha\leq n,
let
L_{\chi,\alpha}
denote the Langlands quotient of the representation
\pi_{\chi,\alpha}.
Then,
(L_{\chi,\alpha})_{N,\psi}\simeq\chi\otimes\chi\nu^{\alpha}
as
\Delta G_{n}
-modules.

Proof.

For each
\alpha\in\{1,\dots,n\},
the segments
\Delta
and
\Delta_{\alpha}
are linked. If
\alpha=n,

\Delta
and
\Delta_{\alpha}
are juxtaposed, so that
Z(\Delta\cup\Delta_{\alpha})
is the character
\chi\nu^{\frac{n}{2}}
of
G_{2n}.
In this case,
\xi_{\chi,n}
(see (
5.5
)) has the unique irreducible quotient
\chi\nu^{\frac{n}{2}}.
By Theorem
5.3
,
(\xi_{\chi,n})_{N,\psi}=\chi\otimes\chi\nu^{n}.
Since,
(\chi\nu^{\frac{n}{2}})_{N,\psi}=0
we get
(\xi_{\chi,n})=(L_{\chi,n})_{N,\psi}=\chi\otimes\chi\nu^{n}.

On the other hand, if
1\leq\alpha<n,

\Delta\cap\Delta_{\alpha}
is non-empty. This yields that
Z(\Delta\cup\Delta_{\alpha})
and
Z(\Delta\cap\Delta_{\alpha})
are both one-dimensional. Again, by Theorem
5.3
,
(Z(\Delta\cup\Delta_{\alpha})\times Z(\Delta\cap\Delta_{\alpha}))_{N,\psi}=0
and
(\xi_{\chi,\alpha})_{N,\psi}\simeq\chi\otimes\chi\nu^{\alpha}
as
\Delta G_{n}
-modules. It follows from the exact sequence (
5.5
) and exactness of the twisted Jacquet functor that
(L_{\chi,\alpha})_{N,\psi}\simeq\chi\otimes\chi\nu^{\alpha}
as
\Delta G_{n}
-modules.
∎

5.3.7.
An application to Shalika Model

As an application of Corollary
5.14
, we deduce the following result on existence of Shalika model for
L_{\chi,\alpha}
when
\chi=\nu^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}.
We recall that a smooth representation
\pi
of
G_{2n}
has a Shalika model if
{\rm{Hom}}_{G_{2n}}(\pi,{\rm{Ind}}_{S_{\psi}}^{G_{2n}}(1\!\!1\otimes\psi))
is non-zero. By Frobenius reciprocity, this is equivalent to saying that

{\rm{Hom}}_{S_{\psi}}(\pi_{{}_{|_{S_{\psi}}}},1\!\!1\otimes\psi)\simeq{\rm{Hom}}_{\Delta G_{n}}(\pi_{N,\psi},1\!\!1)\neq 0,

where
1\!\!1
denotes the trivial character of
\Delta G_{n}.

Corollary 5.15
.

For each
\alpha\in\{1,\dots,n\},
the irreducible representation
L_{\nu^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}},\alpha}
possesses a Shalika model. Consequently, it has a non-zero
G_{n}\times G_{n}
-invariant linear form.

Proof.

Since,
(L_{\nu^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}},\alpha})_{N,\psi}\simeq\mathbb{C},
it is trivial to see that it possesses a Shalika model. It is a result of Jacquet-Rallis
[
14
, Remark pp.117 ]
that any irreducible representation of
G_{2n}
which has a Shalika model has a non-zero
G_{n}\times G_{n}
-invariant linear form.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

We note that
L_{\nu^{-1/2},1}
is the unique irreducible quotient of the representation

St_{2}\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\times\cdots\times St_{2}\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}.

A representation such as
L_{\nu^{-1/2},1}
is an example of a Speh representation in the literature.

For further results on Shalika Models, we refer the reader to the work of N. Matringe
[
16
]
and the references therein.

5.4.
Subquotients of
St_{r}\chi\times\mu

5.4.1.

As another application of our results, we investigate the structure of twisted Jacquet module of subquotients of a parabolically induced representation of the form
St_{r}\cdot\chi\times\mu
where
\chi
and
\mu
are characters of
G_{r}
and
G_{2n-r}
respectively. Put
\Delta=[\chi\nu^{-\frac{r-1}{2}},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{r-1}{2}}]
and
\Delta^{\prime}=[\mu\nu^{-\frac{2n-r-1}{2}},\dots,\mu\nu^{\frac{2n-r-1}{2}}]
so that we can write
St_{r}\cdot\chi\times\mu=L(\Delta)\times Z(\Delta^{\prime}).
As we already know the structure of
(St_{r}\cdot\chi\times\mu)_{N,\psi}
via Proposition
5.2
, we would be interested in determining the structure of the twisted Jacquet modules of the subquotients of
St_{r}\cdot\chi\times\mu
whenever it is reducible.

5.4.2.

We recall
[
2
, Théorème 3.1]
that
St_{r}\cdot\chi\times\mu
is reducible if and only if the segments
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
are juxtaposed, i.e., if and only if
\mu=\chi\nu^{\pm n}.
Moreover, in the case when
St_{r}\cdot\chi\times\mu
reduces, it has length
2
by
[
2
, Remarque 3.2]
. We will assume that
\mu=\chi\nu^{-n}.
The case when
\mu=\chi\nu^{n}
can be treated similarly. As we will be dealing with the character
\nu=|\det(\cdot)|_{F}
of
G_{r}
as
r
varies, we choose to write
\nu_{r}
instead of
\nu
sometimes for avoiding confusion. The trivial character of
G_{n}
shall be denoted by
1\!\!1_{n}.

Proposition 5.17
.

Assume that
r\leq n.
Suppose
\chi
is a character of
F^{\times}.
Let
Z_{\chi,r}
and
Q_{\chi,r}
respectively denote the unique irreducible submodule and the unique irreducible quotient of
St_{r}\chi\times\chi\nu_{2n-r}^{-n}.
The following statements hold:

(1)

If
r<n
then
(Z_{\chi,r})_{N,\psi}=0
and
(Q_{\chi,r})_{N,\psi}=0.

(2)

We have
(Z_{\chi,n})_{N,\psi}=St_{n}\chi\otimes\chi\nu^{-n}
and
(Q_{\chi,n})_{N,\psi}=0.

Proof.

If
r<n,
by Proposition
5.2
(i),
(St_{r}\chi\times\chi\nu_{2n-r}^{-n})_{N,\psi}=0.
Hence (1) follows and we need to consider only the case
r=n.
Put

\Delta=[\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}}]\text{ and }\Delta^{\prime}=[\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}-n},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}-n}],

so that
St_{n}\chi\times\chi\nu_{n}^{-n}=L(\Delta)\times Z(\Delta^{\prime}).

Then, we have the following exact sequence of
G_{2n}
-modules:

(5.6)

0\to Z_{\chi,n}\to St_{n}\chi\times\chi\nu_{n}^{-n}\to Q_{\chi,n}\to 0.

By Proposition
5.2
(ii), we have
(St_{n}\chi\times\chi\nu_{n}^{-n})_{N,\psi}=St_{n}\chi\otimes\chi\nu^{-n}.
We claim that
(Q_{\chi,n})_{N,\psi}=0
which will give us
(Z_{\chi,n})_{N,\psi}=St_{n}\chi\otimes\chi\nu_{n}^{-n}.
Note that
Q_{\chi,n}

=L(\mathfrak{m})
where

\mathfrak{m}=\{[\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}}],[\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}-n}],[\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}-n-1}],\dots,[\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}-n}]\}.

By MW algorithm,
\mathfrak{m}^{t}=\{[\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}}],\dots,[\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-3}{2}}],[\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}-n},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}-n},\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}]\}
and
Q_{\chi,n}=Z(\mathfrak{m}^{t}).
Thus,
Q_{\chi,n}
is a subrepresentation of

\rho\times Z([\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}-n},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}-n},\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}])

where
Z([\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}-n},\dots,\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}-n},\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}])=\chi\nu_{n+1}^{\frac{-n+1}{2}}
is a character of
G_{n+1}
and
\rho=\chi\nu^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\times\times\dots\times\chi\nu^{-\frac{n-3}{2}}\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{n-1}).
By Proposition
5.2
(i),
(\rho\times\nu_{n+1}^{\frac{-n+1}{2}})_{N,\psi}=0
and consequently,
(Q_{\chi,n})_{N,\psi}=0.

∎

Remark 5.18
.

For a smooth representation
\tau
of
G_{n},
let
\tau^{\vee}
denote its contragredient. By considering contragredients of the representations in Proposition
5.17
and the automorphism
s
(
[
31
, §1.9 Theorem & Lemma]
) of
G_{2n}
which maps
P_{r,2n-r}
to
P_{2n-r,r}
we get the exact sequence

0\to Z_{\chi,r}^{\vee}\to\chi^{-1}\nu_{2n-r}^{n}\times St_{r}\chi^{-1}\to Q_{\chi,r}^{\vee}\to 0.

By arguments as in the proof Proposition
5.17
, we may conclude that if
r<n,
then
(Q_{\chi,r}^{\vee})_{N,\psi}=0
and
(Z_{\chi,r}^{\vee})_{N,\psi}=0.
Also, if
r=n,
we have
Q_{\chi,n}^{\vee}
is quotient of the principal series
\chi^{-1}\nu_{n+1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\times\rho^{\vee}.
Hence,
(Q_{\chi,n}^{\vee})_{N,\psi}=0
as
(\chi^{-1}\nu_{n+1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\times\rho^{\vee})_{N,\psi}=0
by Theorem
5.3
. Consequently,
(Z_{\chi,r}^{\vee})_{N,\psi}=\chi^{-1}\nu^{n}\otimes St_{n}\chi^{-1}.

Remark 5.19
.

In contrast to the conclusions of Proposition
5.17
, if
r>n,
both the subquotients of a reducible principal series representation of the type
St_{r}\chi\times\mu
with length
2
can have non-zero twisted Jacquet modules as we shall show in the next subsection. Note that, if
r=n+1,
we already know that such a principal series has a non-zero twisted Jacquet module by Proposition
5.7
.

5.5.
Examples of non-vanishing twisted Jacquet modules I

5.5.1.

In view of the results obtained in Section
5.1
-
5.4
, given any
\pi\in{\rm{Irr}}(G_{2n}),
it would be interesting to know when
\pi_{N,\psi}\neq 0.
To analyze this, we shall consider two reducible principal series representations of
G_{4}
, one in this section and another one the next. In general, if
\xi
is any reducible principal series representation of
G_{2n}
with a non twisted Jacquet module
\xi_{N,\psi},
there may be several subquotients
\pi
with a non-zero
\pi_{N,\psi}.

5.5.2.
Structure theorem for
G_{4}

For the ease of reference, we note the following special case deduced from our main structure theorems by putting
n=r=2
in Corollary
4.15
.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
\chi,\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
be characters of
F^{\times}.
Also, let
\mathcal{G}_{2}
denote the collection of all smooth representations of
G_{2}
consisting of irreducible cuspidal representations, twists of the Steinberg representation
St_{2}\chi
or a (not necessarily irreducible) principal series representation
\chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}.
Assume that
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in{\rm{Alg}}(G_{2}).
Then, the following statements hold:

(1)

If one of the
\rho_{i}
’s is one dimensional,
(\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2})_{N,\psi}=\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}
as
\Delta G_{2}
-modules.

(2)

If both
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{G}_{2},
we have the following exact sequence of
\Delta G_{2}
modules:

(5.7)

0\to\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}\to(\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2})_{N,\psi}\to i_{\Delta B_{2}}^{\Delta G_{2}}(\omega_{\rho_{1}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\omega_{\rho_{2}}\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}})\to 0,

where
\omega_{\rho_{i}}
denotes the central character of the representation
\rho_{i}
for
i=1,2.

Proof.

Apply Corollary
4.15
with
n=r=2
to get the following exact sequence of
\Delta G_{2}
-modules:

(5.8)

0\to\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}\to(\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2})_{N,\psi}\to i_{\Delta B_{2}}^{\Delta GL_{2}(F)}(r_{N_{1,1},\psi_{0}}(\rho_{1})\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes r_{N_{1,1},\psi_{0}}(\rho_{2})\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}})\to 0.

If any
\rho_{i}
is one dimensional,
(1)
follows trivially as
r_{N_{1,1},\psi_{0}}(\rho_{i})=0.
On the other hand, in the hypothesis of
(2)
,
r_{N_{1,1},\psi_{0}}(\rho_{i})=w_{\rho_{i}},
the central character of
\rho_{i}
if
\rho_{i}
is irreducible. The same conclusion holds true for a reducible principal series representation
\chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}
of
G_{2}
as well, since
r_{N_{1,1,},\psi_{0}}(\chi_{1}\times\chi_{2})=\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2}
as
\Delta F^{\times}
-modules by Corollary
4.15
applied with
n=r=1
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.21
.

We note that the exact sequence (
5.7
) is identical to the one of Prasad derived in
[
10
, Propospition 7.1 ]
except the fact that we allow the
\rho_{i}
’s to be reducible principal series representations of
GL_{2}(F)
as well.

5.5.3.

Let
B_{4}
denote the standard Borel subgroup of
G_{4}
and let
\xi:=i_{B_{4}}^{G_{4}}(\delta_{B_{4}}^{1/2})=\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}.
It is well known (see
[
31
, §2.1 Proposition]
and
[
15
, §5.1]
) that the reducible principal series representation
\xi
has
8
irreducible subquotients and each of these appear with multiplicity exactly one in
\xi.
We shall determine precisely which subquotients of
\xi
has a non-zero twisted Jacquet module as a consequence of our results so far. As a first step, we list each one of them as
L(\mathfrak{m})
where
\mathfrak{m}
is a multisegment in which the segments are arranged satisfying does not precede condition. Alternately, it is also fruitful to write them as a Langlands quotient, i.e., as a unique irreducible quotient of an appropriate essentially square integrable representation
\sigma
. By
\mathfrak{L}(\sigma),
we mean the Langlands quotient of
\sigma.

5.5.4.

We briefly deduce the structure of the principal series
\xi.
Let us put
\xi_{{}_{Q}}=\nu_{2}\times\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
and
\xi_{{}_{S}}=St_{2}\nu\times\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
so that we get the exact sequence of
G_{4}
modules

0\to\xi_{{}_{S}}\to\xi\to\xi_{Q}\to 0.

Then,
\xi_{{}_{Q}}
and
\xi_{{}_{S}}
sit in the following two respective exact sequences, namely,

0\to\nu_{2}\times St_{2}\nu^{-1}\to\xi_{{}_{Q}}\to\nu_{2}\times\nu_{2}^{-1}\to 0

and

0\to St_{2}\nu\times St_{2}\nu^{-1}\to\xi_{{}_{S}}\to St_{2}\nu\times\nu_{2}^{-1}\to 0.

From (
5.5
) and (
5.6
), we have the exact sequences

0\to L_{\nu^{-1},2}\to\nu_{2}\times\nu_{2}^{-1}\to 1\!\!1_{4}\to 0

and

0\to Z_{\nu,2}\to St_{2}\nu\times\nu_{2}^{-1}\to Q_{\nu,2}\to 0.

From Remark
5.18
,

0\to Z_{\nu,2}^{\vee}\to\nu_{2}\times St_{2}\nu^{-1}\to Q_{\nu,2}^{\vee}\to 0.

Finally, we have the exact sequence

0\to St_{4}\to St_{2}\nu\times St_{2}\nu^{-1}\to\tau\to 0,

where
\tau=\mathfrak{L}(St_{2}\nu\times St_{2}\nu^{-1}).

We have therefore determined the set
{\rm JH}^{0}(\xi)
consisting of all irreducible subquotients of
\xi
counted with multiplicity. We have

{\rm JH}^{0}(\xi)=\{1\!\!1_{4},L_{\nu^{-1},2},Z_{\nu,2},Q_{\nu_{2}},Z_{\nu,2}^{\vee},Q_{\nu,2}^{\vee},\tau,St_{4}\}.

For this listing of
{\rm JH}^{0}(\xi)
, see also
[
15
, §5.1]
).

5.5.5.

We need the following lemma in the proof of which we shall use the exact sequences in Section
5.5.3
.

Lemma 5.22
.

The unique irreducible quotient
\tau
of the principal series representation
St_{2}\nu\times St_{2}\nu^{-1}
of
G_{4}
has
\tau_{N,\psi}=\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
as
\Delta G_{2}
-modules.

Proof.

The strategy of our proof to compute the structure of
\tau_{N,\psi}
is by realizing
\tau
as a subquotient of another principal series representation
\zeta
such that
\zeta_{N,\psi}
has a simple structure and all irreducible subquotients
\pi
of
\zeta
except
\tau
satisfy
\pi_{N,\psi}=0.
This will yield,
\tau_{N,\psi}=\zeta_{N,\psi}.

Note that
\tau
is a Langlands quotient of
St_{2}\nu\times St_{2}\nu^{-1}.
If we
put
\mathfrak{m}=\{[\nu^{\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}],[\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}},\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}]\},
then
\tau=L(\mathfrak{m}).

By MW algorithm,
\mathfrak{m}^{t}=\{[\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}],[\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}],[\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}]\}.
We then have
\tau=L(\mathfrak{m})=Z(\mathfrak{m}^{t}).
Thus,
\tau
is the unique irreducible submodule of the reducible principal series
\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times 1\!\!1_{2}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}.

Put
\zeta=1\!\!1_{2}\times\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}.
Set
\mathfrak{n}_{1}=\{[\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}},\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}]\},\mathfrak{n}_{2}=\{[\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}],[\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}},\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}]\}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{3}=\{[\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}],[\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}]\}.
By
[
31
, §7.1 Theorem & 9.13 Proposition]
, the irreducible subquotients of
\zeta
and of
\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times 1\!\!1_{2}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
are
\tau=Z(\mathfrak{m}^{t}),Z(\mathfrak{n_{1}}),Z(\mathfrak{n}_{2})
and
Z(\mathfrak{n}_{3}).
Moreover, each of these subquotients appear with multiplicity exactly one. One has
Z(\mathfrak{n}_{1})=1\!\!1_{4}.
By Lemma
5.10
, we have
\mathfrak{n}_{2}^{t}=\{[\nu^{\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}],[\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}],\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\}.
Thus,
Z(\mathfrak{n}_{2})=L(\mathfrak{n}_{2}^{t})=\mathfrak{L}(St_{2}\nu\times\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}})=Q_{\nu,2}.
Apply Lemma
5.10
again to get
\mathfrak{n}_{3}^{t}=\{[\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}],[\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}],[\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}},\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}]\}
so that
Z(\mathfrak{n}_{3})=L(\mathfrak{n}_{3}^{t})=\mathfrak{L}(\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{-1})=Q_{\nu,2}^{\vee}.

By Proposition
5.20
(1),
\zeta_{N,\psi}=1\!\!1_{2}\otimes(\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}})=\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
as
\Delta G_{2}
-modules. By Proposition
5.17
and Remark
5.18
, we have
(Q_{\nu,2})_{N,\psi}=0
and
(Q_{\nu,2}^{\vee})_{N,\psi}=0.
It is trivial that
(1\!\!1_{4})_{N,\psi}=0.
By the exactness of the twisted Jacquet functor,
\tau_{N,\psi}=\zeta_{N,\psi}=\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
as
\Delta G_{2}
-modules.
∎

Corollary 5.23
.

The principal series representation
St_{3}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
of
G_{4}
has length
2
with unique irreducible quotient
Z_{\nu,2}
and unique irreducible subrepresentation
St_{4}.
Both the twisted Jacquet modules
(Z_{\nu,2})_{N,\psi}
and
(St_{4})_{N,\psi}
are non-zero.

Proof.

It is well known that
St_{3}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
has length 2 and
St_{4}
is its unique irreducible subrepresentation. Let
\eta
be the unique irreducible quotient of
St_{3}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}.
We shall first show that
\eta=Z_{\nu,2}.
Put
\mathfrak{m}=\{[\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{1}{2}},\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}],[\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}]\}
so that
\mathfrak{m}^{t}=\{[\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}],[\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}],[\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}},\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}]\}
by MW algorithm. We have
\eta=L(\mathfrak{m})=Z(\mathfrak{m}^{t}).

Thus,
\eta
is the unique irreducible submodule of the principal series representation
\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu_{2}^{-1}.
Note that,
St_{2}\nu\times\nu_{2}^{-1}\hookrightarrow\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu_{2}^{-1}
and therefore
\eta
is the unique irreducible submodule of
St_{2}\nu\times\nu_{2}^{-1}
as well. Hence,
\eta=Z_{\nu,2}.
By Proposition
5.17
(2), we have
(Z_{\nu,2})_{N,\psi}=St_{2}\nu^{-1}\otimes\nu_{2}^{-1}
as
\Delta G_{2}
-modules.

The principal series
St_{2}\nu^{-1}\times St_{2}\nu
has length
2.
Its irreducible subquotients are
St_{4}
and the representation
\tau
of Lemma
5.22
. By Proposition
5.20
(2),
(St_{2}\nu\times St_{2}\nu^{-1})_{N,\psi}
is glued from
St_{2}\nu\otimes St_{2}\nu^{-1}=St_{2}\otimes St_{2}
and the irreducible principal series representation
i_{\Delta B_{2}}^{\Delta GL_{2}(F)}(\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\otimes\nu^{\frac{3}{2}})\cong\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}.
Of these,
\tau_{N,\psi}
accounts for
\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
by Lemma
5.22
, proving
(St_{4})_{N,\psi}
must be non-zero.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

Corollary
5.23
provides an example for the case mentioned in Remark
5.19
, where both irreducible subquotients of a reducible principal series representation
St_{r}\chi\times\mu
can have non-zero twisted Jacquet modules if
r>n.

5.5.6.
Subquotients
\pi
of
\xi
and non-vanishing of
\pi_{N,\psi}

We tabulate the findings about the irreducible subquotients of
\xi
in Table 1.

5.6.
Examples of non-vanishing twisted Jacquet modules II

5.6.1.

Consider the principal series
\sigma=\nu\times 1\times 1\times\nu^{-1}
of
G_{4}.
To contrast with the principal series
\xi
considered in Section
5.5
, the cuspidal support of
\xi
is distinct where as the cuspidal support of
\sigma
has the character
1
appearing with multiplicity
2.
The subquotients of
\sigma
are well-known as it is a special case of
[
31
, Example 11.4]
. By
[
31
]
, there are five distinct irreducible representations of
\sigma
of which exactly one subquotient appears with multiplicity
2
in
\sigma
and all the remaining four subquotients appear with multiplicity one. We shall explain this below.

5.6.2.

Put
\sigma_{{}_{S}}=St_{2}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times 1\times\nu^{-1}
and
\sigma_{{}_{Q}}=\nu_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\times 1\times\nu^{-1}.
To begin with,
\sigma
sits in the following exact sequence of
G_{4}
-modules:

0\to\sigma_{S}\to\sigma\to\sigma_{Q}\to 0.

The representations
\sigma_{{}_{S}}
and
\sigma_{{}_{Q}}
sit in the following exact sequences:

0\to St_{2}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\to\sigma_{{}_{S}}\to St_{2}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\to 0

and

0\to\nu_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\to\sigma_{{}_{Q}}\to\nu_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\to 0.

If we take
\Delta=[\nu^{-1},1]
and
\Delta^{\prime}=[1,\nu]
then we can analyze the principal series
\sigma,\sigma_{{}_{S}}
and
\sigma_{{}_{Q}}
in a straightforward manner. Note that
\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}=[\nu^{-1},1,\nu],\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}=[1]
and so
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
are linked but not juxtaposed. Therefore,
St_{2}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is irreducible by
[
2
, Théorème 3.1]
. The same argument applies to
\nu_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Put
n=2,\alpha=1
and
\chi=\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in Lemma
5.11
to get

0\to L_{\nu^{-1/2},1}\to\nu_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\to 1\!\!1_{3}\times 1\to 0.

By Lemma
5.11
, we also have
L_{\nu^{-1/2},1}
is the Langlands quotient of
St_{2}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}.
This gives us,

0\to St_{3}\times 1\to St_{2}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\to L_{\nu^{-1/2},1}\to 0.

The representations,
St_{3}\times 1
and
1\!\!1_{3}\times 1
are irreducible as the segments
\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}
and
\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}
are not linked. Thus, the irreducible subquotients of
\sigma
are

St_{2}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}},\nu_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}},1\!\!1_{3}\times 1,St_{3}\times 1\mbox{ and }L_{\nu^{-1/2},1}.

Of these,
L_{\nu^{-1/2},1}
appears with multiplicity
2
(cf.
[
31
, §11.4 Proposition]
).

5.6.3.
Subquotients
\pi
of
\xi
and non-vanishing of
\pi_{N,\psi}

By Proposition
5.17
and Remark
5.18
, both
(St_{2}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}})_{N,\psi}
and
(\nu_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}})_{N,\psi}
are non-zero. By Proposition
5.7
,
(St_{3}\times 1)_{N,\psi}
is non-zero as well. By Corollary
5.15
,
(L_{\nu^{-1/2},1})_{N,\psi}
is also non-zero. Finally, by Theorem
5.3
,
(1\!\!1_{3}\times 1)_{N,\psi}=0.
Thus, the only subquotient of
\sigma
whose twisted Jacquet module vanishes is
1\!\!1_{3}\times 1.
We have the following Table 2.

Subquotient
\pi
of
\sigma

\pi_{N,\psi}

1\!\!1_{3}\times 1

zero

St_{2}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

non-zero

\nu_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

non-zero

L_{\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}},1}

non-zero
