6.
Non vanishing of twisted Jacquet modules: a conjecture

Based on the results of this work, D. Prasad has proposed Conjecture
6.3
on non-vanishing of twisted Jacquet module
\pi_{N,\psi}
for irreducible admissible representations
\pi
of
G_{2n}
where
(N,\psi)
is as in (
1.1
) and everywhere else in this work. This conjecture is based especially on the assertions contained in this work.

In Section
6.1
, we summarize some facts on Langlands parameters and
L
-functions necessary to state Prasad’s conjecture. In Section
6.3
, we verify Conjecture
6.3
holds in few cases using the results we have proved in previous sections. Section
6.4
lists some cases where we have not verified Conjecture
6.3
in full generality.

6.1.
Short summary on Langlands parameters and
L
-functions

We shall use the notion of
L
-functions in this section, especially the adjoint
L
-function of an irreducible admissible representation of
G_{n}.

Before we proceed further, we shall summarize a few facts especially from
[
15
, Section 3]
for ease of reference. For notations and details, we refer the reader to
[
15
]
.

6.1.1.

Suppose
\pi
is an irreducible smooth representation of
G_{n}
and
\rho={\rm rec}_{F}^{n}(\pi)
is its Langlands parameter, then the adjoint
L
-function of
\pi
is
L(s,Ad\circ\pi)=L(s,\rho\otimes\rho^{\vee}).

6.1.2.

For a one dimensional representation,
\chi
of
F^{\times},
we shall identify
\chi
with
rec_{F}^{1}(\chi)
which is a character of
W_{F}^{ab}.
The
L
-function of
\chi
is
(1-\chi(\omega_{F})q^{-s})^{-1}
where
\omega_{F}
denotes the uniformizer of
F.
In particular, if
\chi=|\cdot|_{F}^{t},
where
t\in\mathbb{R},
the only pole on the real line of the
L
-function of
\chi
is a simple pole at
s=-t.

6.1.3.

The Steinberg representation
St_{n}
has as its
L
-function
L(s,|\cdot|_{F}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}).
For any twist
St_{n}\chi
of the Steinberg representation, one has
L(s,St_{n}\chi)=L(s,\chi|\cdot|_{F}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}).
In view of this, any real pole of
L(s,St_{n}\chi)
is a simple pole and for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
L(s,St_{n}|\cdot|_{F}^{t})
has a pole in the region
s>0
if and only if
t<-\frac{r-1}{2}.

6.1.4.

If
\rho_{2}
denotes the Langlands parameter of
St_{2},
then
\rho_{2}\otimes\rho_{2}=1\!\!1_{1}\oplus\rho_{3}
where
\rho_{3}
is the Langlands parameter of the Steinberg representation
St_{3}
of
G_{3}.
By abuse of notation, we shall write this as
St_{2}\otimes St_{2}=1\!\!1_{1}\oplus St_{3}
in our calculations. More generally, if
\rho_{r}
denotes the Langlands parameter of the Steinberg representation
St_{r}
of
G_{r},
one has
\rho_{r}\otimes\rho_{r}=1\!\!1_{1}\oplus\rho_{3}\oplus\cdots\oplus\rho_{2r-1}.

6.1.5.

Assume that
\tau_{j}=L(\Delta_{j})
are essentially square integrable irreducible smooth representations of
G_{n_{j}}
for
1\leq j\leq k
such that
\Delta_{i}
does not precede
\Delta_{j}
whenever
i<j.
Then,
\tau_{1}\times\cdots\times\tau_{k}
has a unique irreducible quotient
\pi
which is also the Langlands quotient and
L(s,\pi)=\prod_{j=1,\dots,k}L(s,\tau_{j})
(
[
15
, (3.1.3)]
).

6.1.6.

Suppose
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are irreducible smooth representations of
G_{m}
and
G_{n}
respectively with Langlands parameters
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
such that
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}
is irreducible, then (see
[
29
, A.6 Corollary(ii)]
) the Langlands parameter of
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}
is
\rho_{1}\oplus\rho_{2}.
We shall use this repeatedly in our calculations.

6.1.7.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N},
the trivial representation
1\!\!1_{k}
of
G_{k}
has Langlands parameter
\nu^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\nu^{-(\frac{k-1}{2})}.
By
6.1.6
, we have

1\!\!1_{k}\otimes 1\!\!1_{k}=(\nu^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\nu^{-\frac{k-1}{2}})\otimes(\nu^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\nu^{-\frac{k-1}{2}}),

and therefore

(6.1)

L(s,1\!\!1_{k}\otimes 1\!\!1_{k})=\nu^{k-1}\oplus 2\nu^{k-2}\oplus\cdots\oplus(k-1)\nu\oplus k1\!\!1_{1}\oplus(k-1)\nu^{-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\nu^{-(k-1)}.

Consequently,

L(s,Ad,1\!\!1_{k})
has poles of order
1,2,\dots,k-1
at
s=k-1,k-2,\dots,1
respectively.

6.2.
D. Prasad’s Conjecture

We recall the following result from
[
15
, Proposition 5.2.2]
which is a motivation for Conjecture
6.3
. We recall that
Ad:GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})\to GL(M_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
denotes the adjoint representation of
GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

Theorem 6.1

(Gross, D. Prasad, Rallis)
.

Let
\pi
be an irreducible smooth representation of
G_{n}
and let
\rho={\rm rec}_{F}^{n}(\pi)
denote its Langlands parameter.
Then
\pi
is generic if and only if
L(s,\pi,Ad)=L(s,Ad\circ\rho)=L(s,\rho\otimes\rho^{\vee})
has no pole at
s=1.

In other words, Theorem
6.1
says that the twisted Jacquet module
\pi_{U_{n},\theta}
(see Section
5.2
) is zero if and only if
L(s,\rho\otimes\rho^{\vee})
has a pole at
s=1.
Conjecture
6.3
formulated below can be viewed as an analogue of Theorem
6.1
for
\pi_{N,\psi}.

Remark 6.2
.

According to
[
11
, Conjecture 2.6]
, a statement as in Theorem
6.1
is expected to be true for more general reductive groups than
G_{n}.

Conjecture 6.3

(D. Prasad)
.

Let
\pi
be an irreducible smooth representation of
G_{2n}
and let
\rho={\rm rec}_{F}^{2n}(\pi)
denote its Langlands parameter. Then, the twisted Jacquet module
\pi_{N,\psi}=0
if and only if the
L
-function
L(s,\pi,Ad)=L(s,\rho\otimes\rho^{\vee})
has poles of order greater than or equal to
n,n-1,\dots,1
at
s=1,2,\dots,n
respectively.

In the following Sections
6.3
and
6.4
, by abuse of notation, we shall identify a smooth irreducible representation
\pi
of
G_{n}
with its Langlands parameter
rec_{F}^{n}(\pi)
for the purposes of calculation of the adjoint
L
-function. We hope this eases notation and avoids some tedious book keeping.

6.3.
Special cases of the conjecture

6.3.1.

Assume that
\pi
is an irreducible smooth representation of
G_{2n}
of the form
\pi=1\!\!1_{n+1}\times\sigma
where
1\!\!1_{n+1}
denotes the trivial representation of
G_{n+1}
and
\sigma
is any irreducible representation of
G_{n-1}.
By
6.1.6
,
\pi\otimes\pi^{\vee}
contains
1\!\!1_{n+1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{n+1}
and by (
6.1
),
L(s,1\!\!1_{n+1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{n+1})
has poles at
s=1,2,\dots,n
of order
n,n-1,\dots,1
respectively. Thus, by the Conjecture
6.3
,
\pi_{N,\psi}=0.
Also, by Proposition
5.1
(1),
\pi_{N,\psi}=0.

6.3.2.

Assume that
\pi
is a smooth representation of
G_{2n}
of the form
\pi=1\!\!1_{n-1}\times\sigma
where
1\!\!1_{n-1}
denotes the trivial representation of
G_{n-1}
and
\sigma
is any irreducible generic representation of
G_{n+1}
which we also assume to be tempered. In particular,
\pi
is irreducible. Then, by
6.1.6

\displaystyle\pi\otimes\pi^{\vee}=1\!\!1_{n-1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{n-1}\oplus\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\vee}\oplus 1\!\!1_{n-1}\otimes\sigma\oplus 1\!\!1_{n-1}\otimes\sigma^{\vee}.

Since,
\sigma
is assumed to be tempered neither
1\!\!1\otimes\sigma
and
1\!\!1\otimes\sigma^{\vee}
nor
\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\vee}
have a pole in the region
s>0.
Therefore, all the poles of
L(s,\pi\otimes\pi^{\vee})
are contributed by
L(s,1\!\!1_{n-1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{n-1}).
By (
6.1
),
L(s,1\!\!1_{n-1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{n-1})
has poles at
s=1,2,\dots,n-1
of order
n-1,n-2,\dots,1
respectively. Therefore,
\pi_{N,\psi}\neq 0
by Conjecture
6.3
, and this is the case by Proposition
5.7
(2).

6.3.3.

The representation
\tau
of
G_{4}
constructed in Section
5.5
with Langlands parameter
St_{2}\nu\oplus St_{2}\nu^{-1}.
In this case, we have

\displaystyle\tau\otimes\tau^{\vee}=

\displaystyle(St_{2}\nu\oplus{\mathrm{St}}_{2}\nu^{-1})\otimes(St_{2}\nu\oplus St_{2}\nu^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(St_{2}\otimes St_{2})\otimes(\nu^{2}\oplus 1\!\!1_{1}\oplus 1\!\!1_{1}\oplus\nu^{-2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1\!\!1_{1}\oplus St_{3})\otimes(\nu^{2}\oplus 1\!\!1_{1}\oplus 1\!\!1_{1}\oplus\nu^{-2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{2}\oplus 1\!\!1_{1}\oplus 1\!\!1_{1}\oplus\nu^{-2}\oplus St_{3}\nu^{2}\oplus St_{3}\oplus St_{3}\oplus St_{3}\nu^{-2}

In this case, for
L(s,\tau\otimes\tau^{\vee})
, the poles in the region
s>0
are at
s=2
of order 1 contributed by
L(s,\nu^{-2})
and at
s=1
of order 1 (and not 2) contributed by
L(s,St_{3}\nu^{-2}).
Thus, by Conjecture
6.3
, the representation
\tau
of
G_{4}
has non-zero twisted Jacquet module which is also the case by Lemma
5.22
. In fact, Conjecture
6.3
is inspired by this particular example.

6.3.4.

The representations
Q_{\nu,2}
and
Q_{\nu,2}^{\vee}
which are Langlands quotients of
St_{2}\nu\times\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}
and
\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\times\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times St_{2}\nu^{-1}
respectively have twisted Jacquet module equals zero by Section
5.5
. The adjoint representation of these representations decomposes into

St_{3}\oplus 3\cdot 1\!\!1_{1}\oplus St_{2}\otimes(\nu^{\frac{5}{2}}\oplus\nu^{\frac{3}{2}}\oplus\nu^{\frac{-3}{2}}\oplus\nu^{\frac{-5}{2}})\oplus\nu\oplus\nu^{-1}.

The poles in the region
s>0
of the adjoint
L
-function are therefore at
s=1
and
s=2
of order
2
and
1
respectively. By Conjecture
6.3
, the twisted Jacquet modules under consideration are zero as well.

6.3.5.

Consider the representation
Q_{1,r}
appearing in Proposition
5.17
. We write
Q_{1,r}=St_{r}\oplus\nu_{2n-r}^{n}.
Then, one has

Q_{1,r}\otimes Q_{1,r}^{\vee}=St_{r}\otimes St_{r}\oplus St_{r}\otimes\nu_{2n-r}^{n}\oplus St_{r}\otimes\nu_{n}^{-n}\oplus 1\!\!1_{2n-r}\otimes 1\!\!1_{2n-r}.

Of these,
L(s,St_{r}\otimes St_{r})
does not contribute any poles in
s>0.
Also, it is not difficult to see that
L(s,St_{r}\otimes\nu_{2n-r}^{n})
does not contribute any pole in the region
s>0
as well. On the other hand, by (
6.1
),
L(s,1\!\!1_{2n-r}\otimes 1\!\!1_{2n-r})
has poles of order
1,2,\dots,2n-r-1
at
s=2n-r-1,2n-r-2,\dots,1
respectively. Moreover,

L(s,St_{r}\otimes\nu_{2n-r}^{-n})=L(s,\nu^{r-2n})L(s,\nu^{r-2n-1})\cdots L(s,\nu^{-1}).

If
r\leq n,

2n-r\geq n
and
2n-r-1\geq n-1.
Thus, counting the order of poles of both
L(s,St_{r}\otimes\nu_{2n-r}^{-n})
and
L(s,1\!\!1_{2n-r}\otimes 1\!\!1_{2n-r})
, we have
L(s,Q_{1,r}\otimes Q_{1,r}^{\vee})
has a pole of order greater than or equal to
n,n-1,\dots,1
at
s=1,2,\dots,n
respectively. By Conjecture
6.3
,
(Q_{1,r})_{N,\psi}=0
which agrees with Proposition
5.17
.

6.3.6.

Consider the representation
Z_{1,r}
appearing in Proposition
5.17
. By Proposition
5.17
, we have
(Z_{1,r})_{N,\psi}=0
if
r<n
and
(Z_{1,r})_{N,\psi}
is non-zero if
r=n.
We shall verify below that Conjecture
6.3
holds for
Z_{1,r}
as well.

Note that
Z_{1,r}=Z(\mathfrak{m})
where

\mathfrak{m}=\{[\nu^{\frac{r-1}{2}}],[\nu^{\frac{r-3}{2}}],\dots,[\nu^{-(\frac{r-1}{2})}],\Delta\}
where
\Delta=[\nu^{-\frac{2n-r-1}{2}}\cdot\nu^{-n},\dots,\nu^{\frac{2n-r-1}{2}}\cdot\nu^{-n}].
Note that,
\Delta
ends at
\nu^{-\frac{r+1}{2}}.
Applying the MW algorithm to
\mathfrak{m},
we get

\mathfrak{m}^{t}=\{\Delta^{\prime},[\nu^{-\frac{r+3}{2}}],\dots,[\nu^{-\frac{-(2n-r-1)}{2}}\cdot\nu^{-n}]\}
where
\Delta^{\prime}=[\nu^{-\frac{r+1}{2}},\dots,\nu^{\frac{r-1}{2}}].
Consequently,
Z_{1,r}=L(\mathfrak{m}^{t})
yielding that
Z_{1,r}
is a Langlands quotient of
St_{r+1}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\nu^{-\frac{r+3}{2}}\times\cdots\times\nu^{-\frac{2n-r-1}{2}}\cdot\nu^{-n}.
Noting that the unique irreducible quotient of
\nu^{-\frac{r+3}{2}}\times\cdots\times\nu^{-\frac{2n-r-1}{2}}\cdot\nu^{-n}
is the character
\nu_{2n-r-1}^{-n-\frac{1}{2}},
we shall write for the purpose of computing the adjoint
L
-function that
Z_{1,r}=St_{r+1}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\nu_{2n-r-1}^{-n-\frac{1}{2}}.
Note that

Z_{1,r}\otimes Z_{1,r}^{\vee}=St_{r+1}\otimes St_{r+1}\oplus St_{r+1}\otimes\nu_{2n-r-1}^{n+1}\oplus St_{r+1}\otimes\nu_{2n-r-1}^{-n-1}\oplus 1\!\!1_{2n-r-1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{2n-r-1}.

Arguing as in previous example, the first two factors in the direct sum do not contribute to poles in the region
s>0.
We need only consider the poles contributed by
L(s,St_{r+1}\otimes\nu_{2n-r-1}^{-n-1})
and
L(s,1\!\!1_{2n-r-1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{2n-r-1}).
By (
6.1
),
L(s,1\!\!1_{2n-r-1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{2n-r-1})
has poles at
s=1,2,\dots,2n-r-2
of order
2n-r-2,2n-r-3,\dots,1
respectively whereas,

L(s,St_{r+1}\otimes\nu_{2n-r-1}^{-n-1})=L(s,\nu^{r-2n})L(s,\nu^{r-2n-1})\cdots L(s,\nu^{n-2}).

We split the further verification into two cases naturally.

Case
1: Assume that
r=n.
Here,
L(s,St_{n+1}\otimes\nu_{n-1}^{-n-1})
has a pole of order
1
at
s=1
and
(L(s,1\!\!1_{n-1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{n-1})
has a pole of order
n-2
at
s=1
yielding that the order of the pole at
s=1
is
n-1
and not
n.
Hence,
(Z_{1,n})_{N,\psi}\neq 0.

Case
2: Assume that
r<n.
In this case,
r-2n<-n,

2n-r\geq n+1
and hence
2n-r-2\geq n-1.
The factor
L(s,St_{r+1}\otimes\nu_{2n-r-1}^{-n-1}))
has a simple pole at
s=1,2,\dots,n
as
\{\nu^{-n},\nu^{-(n-1)},\dots,1\}
is contained in
\{\nu^{r-2n},\dots,\nu^{n-2}\}.
As
2n-r-2\geq n-1,
from
L(s,1\!\!1_{2n-r-1}\otimes 1\!\!1_{2n-r-1})
we get a pole of order
\geq 1
at
s=n-1,
a pole of order
\geq 2
at
s=n-2
,
\dots
, a pole of order
\geq n-1
at
s=1.
Combining the contribution from both the factors,
L(s,Z_{1,r}\otimes Z_{1,r}^{\vee})
has a pole of order
\geq 1
at
s=n,
pole of order
\geq 2
at
s=n-1,

\dots,
a pole of order
\geq n
at
s=1.
Thus,
(Z_{1,r})_{N,\psi}=0.

In both the cases, Conjecture
6.3
agrees with Proposition
5.17
.

6.4.
Some other cases of the conjecture

Here are few particular cases of the conjecture we have not proved in this paper.

6.4.1.

In view of Theorem
6.1
, Conjecture
6.3
is expected to hold for all irreducible generic representations of
G_{2n}.

6.4.2.

The representation
\pi=1\!\!1_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times 1\!\!1_{n_{m}}
of
G_{2n}
where
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{m}=2n
with
n_{j}\leq n
has a nontrivial twisted Jacquet module as their adjoint
L
-function do not have a pole at
s=n.

6.4.3.

Suppose
n,r,m
are positive integers such that
2n=rm.
Let
\tau
be any discrete series representation (see
[
15
, §1.2, §2.4]
,
[
29
]
) of
G_{m}.
The induced representation
\tau\nu^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\times\cdots\times\tau\nu^{-\frac{r-1}{2}}
has a unique irreducible quotient denoted by
u(\tau,r),
known as a Speh representation. Such a representation is unitary (
[
29
, Theorem D, pp. 338]
,
[
15
, §2.4]
) and by Conjecture
6.3
,
u(\tau,r)
has a non-zero twisted Jacquet module unless it is a one dimensional representation.

Remark 6.4
.

We note the following generality which is applicable to verify some cases of Conjecture
6.3
, especially to representations of the type in
6.4.2
. Suppose
k>1
and
\pi\in{\rm{Irr}}(G_{2n})
is such that
\pi=\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{k}
where for all
j,

\pi_{j}=Z(\Delta_{j})\in{\rm{Irr}}(G_{n_{j}})
for some segments
\Delta_{j}
and
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=2n.
Assume that there exists
n_{j_{1}},\dots,n_{j_{t}}
among the
n_{i}
’s such that
n_{j_{1}}+\cdots+n_{j_{t}}=n.
Then we can rearrange the
n_{j}
’s and assume without loss of generality that
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{t}=n.
We may then apply Proposition
5.2
to
\pi=(\pi_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n_{t}})\times(\pi_{n_{t+1}}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n_{m}})
to get
\pi_{N,\psi}\neq 0.
A similar argument works for representations
\pi=L(\Delta_{1})\times\cdots\times L(\Delta_{k}),
which are in particular generic.
